**Adolf Hitler**[a] (20 April 1889 - 30 April 1945) was an Austrian-born German politician who was the [dictator](/wiki/Dictator "Dictator") of [Germany during the Nazi era](/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") from 1933 until [his suicide](/wiki/Death_of_Adolf_Hitler "Death of Adolf Hitler") in 1945. [He rose to power](/wiki/Adolf_Hitler%27s_rise_to_power "Adolf Hitler's rise to power") as the leader of the [Nazi Party](/wiki/Nazi_Party "Nazi Party"),[b] becoming the [chancellor of Germany](/wiki/Chancellor_of_Germany#Nazi_Germany_\(1933–1945\) "Chancellor of Germany") in 1933 and then taking the title of _[Führer und Reichskanzler](/wiki/F%C3%BChrer_und_Reichskanzler "Führer und Reichskanzler")_ in 1934.[c] Germany's [invasion of Poland](/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland "Invasion of Poland") on 1 September 1939 under his leadership marked the outbreak of the [Second World War](/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War"). Throughout the ensuing conflict, Hitler was closely involved in the direction of German military operations and was central to the perpetration of [the Holocaust](/wiki/The_Holocaust "The Holocaust"), the [genocide](/wiki/Genocide "Genocide") of [about six million Jews and millions of other victims](/wiki/Victims_of_Nazi_Germany "Victims of Nazi Germany"). 

Hitler was born in [Braunau am Inn](/wiki/Braunau_am_Inn "Braunau am Inn") in [Austria-Hungary](/wiki/Austria-Hungary "Austria-Hungary") and moved to [Germany](/wiki/German_Empire "German Empire") in 1913. He was decorated during his service in the German Army in the [First World War](/wiki/First_World_War "First World War"), receiving the [Iron Cross](/wiki/Iron_Cross "Iron Cross"). In 1919, he joined the [German Workers' Party](/wiki/German_Workers%27_Party "German Workers' Party") (DAP), the precursor of the Nazi Party, and in 1921, was appointed the leader of the Nazi Party. In 1923, he attempted to seize governmental power in [a failed coup](/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch "Beer Hall Putsch") in Munich and was sentenced to five years in prison, serving just over a year. While there, he dictated the first volume of his autobiography and political manifesto _[Mein Kampf](/wiki/Mein_Kampf "Mein Kampf")_ (lit. 'My Struggle'). After his early release in 1924, he gained popular support by attacking the [Treaty of Versailles](/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles "Treaty of Versailles") as well as promoting [pan-Germanism](/wiki/Pan-Germanism "Pan-Germanism"), [antisemitism](/wiki/Antisemitism "Antisemitism"), and [anti-communism](/wiki/Anti-communism "Anti-communism") with [charismatic](/wiki/Charismatic_authority "Charismatic authority") oratory and [Nazi propaganda](/wiki/Propaganda_in_Nazi_Germany "Propaganda in Nazi Germany"). He frequently denounced [communism](/wiki/Communism "Communism") as being part of an [international Jewish conspiracy](/wiki/International_Jewish_conspiracy "International Jewish conspiracy"). 

By November 1932, the Nazi Party held the most seats in the _[Reichstag](/wiki/Reichstag_\(Weimar_Republic\) "Reichstag \(Weimar Republic\)")_ , but not a majority. Former chancellor [Franz von Papen](/wiki/Franz_von_Papen "Franz von Papen") and other conservative politicians convinced President [Paul von Hindenburg](/wiki/Paul_von_Hindenburg "Paul von Hindenburg") to appoint Hitler as chancellor on 30 January 1933. Shortly thereafter, on 23 March, the _Reichstag_ passed the [Enabling Act of 1933](/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1933 "Enabling Act of 1933"), which ultimately began the [Weimar Republic](/wiki/Weimar_Republic "Weimar Republic")'s transformation into Nazi Germany. Upon Hindenburg's death on 2 August 1934, Hitler replaced him as [head of state](/wiki/Head_of_state "Head of state") and thereafter transformed Germany into a [totalitarian](/wiki/Totalitarian "Totalitarian") dictatorship. Domestically, Hitler implemented numerous [racist policies](/wiki/Racial_policies_of_the_Third_Reich "Racial policies of the Third Reich") and sought to deport or kill [German Jews](/wiki/German_Jews "German Jews"). His first six years in power resulted in rapid economic recovery from the [Great Depression](/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression"), the abrogation of restrictions imposed on Germany after the First World War, and the [annexation](/wiki/Annexation "Annexation") of territories inhabited by millions of ethnic Germans, which initially gave him significant popular support. 

One of Hitler's key goals was _[Lebensraum](/wiki/Lebensraum "Lebensraum")_ (lit. 'living space') for the German people in Eastern Europe. His aggressive, [expansionist](/wiki/Expansionism "Expansionism") foreign policy is considered the primary [cause of World War II in Europe](/wiki/Causes_of_World_War_II "Causes of World War II"). On 1 September 1939, Hitler oversaw the German invasion of Poland, thereby causing Britain and [France](/wiki/French_Third_Republic "French Third Republic") to [declare war on Germany](/wiki/Declarations_of_war_during_World_War_II "Declarations of war during World War II"). After ordering [an invasion of the Soviet Union](/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa "Operation Barbarossa") in June 1941, he [declared war on the United States](/wiki/German_declaration_of_war_against_the_United_States "German declaration of war against the United States") in December of the same year. By the end of 1941, German forces and the European [Axis powers](/wiki/Axis_powers "Axis powers") occupied most of Europe and [North Africa](/wiki/North_African_campaign "North African campaign"). These gains were gradually reversed after 1941 until the [Allied forces](/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II "Allies of World War II") defeated the German military in 1945. On 29 April 1945, Hitler married his longtime partner, [Eva Braun](/wiki/Eva_Braun "Eva Braun"), in the _[Führerbunker](/wiki/F%C3%BChrerbunker "Führerbunker")_ in Berlin. They committed suicide the next day to avoid capture by the Soviet [Red Army](/wiki/Red_Army "Red Army"). 

The historian and biographer [Ian Kershaw](/wiki/Ian_Kershaw "Ian Kershaw") described Hitler as "the embodiment of modern political evil".[3] Under Hitler's leadership and [racist ideology](/wiki/Nazi_racial_theories "Nazi racial theories"), the Nazi regime was responsible for the genocide of an estimated six million Jews and millions of other victims, whom he and his followers deemed _[Untermenschen](/wiki/Untermensch "Untermensch")_ (lit. 'subhumans') or socially undesirable. Hitler and the Nazis were also responsible for the deliberate killing of an estimated 19.3 million civilians and prisoners of war. In addition, 28.7 million soldiers and civilians died as a result of military action in the European [theatre](/wiki/Theater_\(warfare\) "Theater \(warfare\)"). The number of [civilians killed during World War II](/wiki/World_War_II_casualties "World War II casualties") was unprecedented in warfare, and the casualties make it the [deadliest conflict in history](/wiki/List_of_wars_by_death_toll "List of wars by death toll"). 

## Ancestry

See also: [Hitler family](/wiki/Hitler_family "Hitler family")

Hitler's father, [Alois Hitler](/wiki/Alois_Hitler "Alois Hitler"), was the [illegitimate](/wiki/Legitimacy_\(family_law\) "Legitimacy \(family law\)") child of [Maria Schicklgruber](/wiki/Maria_Schicklgruber "Maria Schicklgruber").[4] The baptismal register did not show the name of his father, and Alois initially bore his mother's surname, "Schicklgruber". In 1842, [Johann Georg Hiedler](/wiki/Johann_Georg_Hiedler "Johann Georg Hiedler") married Alois's mother. Alois was brought up in the family of Hiedler's brother, [Johann Nepomuk Hiedler](/wiki/Johann_Nepomuk_Hiedler "Johann Nepomuk Hiedler").[5] Alois worked as a civil servant from 1855 until his retirement in 1895.[6] In 1876, Alois was made legitimate and his baptismal record annotated by a priest to register Johann Georg Hiedler as Alois's father (recorded as "Georg Hitler").[7][8] Alois then assumed the surname "Hitler",[8] also spelled "Hiedler", "Hüttler", or "Huettler". The name is probably based on the German word _Hütte_ (lit. 'hut'), and has the meaning "one who lives in a hut".[9]

The Nazi official [Hans Frank](/wiki/Hans_Frank "Hans Frank") suggested that Alois's mother had been employed as a housekeeper by a Jewish family in [Graz](/wiki/Graz "Graz"), and that the family's 19-year-old son Leopold Frankenberger had fathered Alois, a claim that came to be known as the [Frankenberger thesis](/wiki/Frankenberger_thesis "Frankenberger thesis").[10] No Frankenberger was registered in Graz during that period, and no record has been produced of a Leopold Frankenberger's existence,[11] so historians dismiss the claim that Alois's father was Jewish.[12][13] In 2025, blood from the sofa in Hitler's study was used by [Turi King](/wiki/Turi_King "Turi King") of the University of Bath for [DNA analysis](/wiki/DNA_analysis "DNA analysis"). The blood was confirmed to be Hitler's by comparing it to that of a male-line relative, and the DNA analysis also showed that Hitler did not have Jewish ancestry through his father. "If that was the case, we wouldn't have got the DNA match with him," King said. "That DNA match not only confirmed that this is Hitler's DNA but also confirms that the story of human Jewish ancestry through his father is just simply not true."[14][15]

## Early life

### Childhood and education

[](/wiki/File:Adolf_Hitler_infant_photo_\(3x4_cropped\).jpg)Hitler (c. 1889–90)

Adolf Hitler was born on 20 April 1889 in [Braunau am Inn](/wiki/Braunau_am_Inn "Braunau am Inn"), a town in [Austria-Hungary](/wiki/Austria-Hungary "Austria-Hungary") (present-day Austria), close to the border with Germany.[16][17] He was the fourth of six children born to Alois Hitler and his third wife, [Klara Pölzl](/wiki/Klara_P%C3%B6lzl "Klara Pölzl"). Three of Hitler's siblings—Gustav, Ida, and Otto—died in infancy.[18] Also living in the household were Alois's children from his second marriage: Alois Jr. (born 1882) and [Angela](/wiki/Angela_Hitler "Angela Hitler") (born 1883).[19] In 1892, the family moved to [Passau](/wiki/Passau "Passau"), Germany, following Alois's promotion to the customs administration in Passau. Hitler was three at the time. Alois was promoted and transferred to [Linz](/wiki/Linz "Linz"), Austria, on 1 April 1893, but the rest of the family remained in Passau.[20] There, Hitler acquired the distinctive [lower Bavarian dialect](/wiki/Bavarian_language "Bavarian language"), rather than [Austrian German](/wiki/Austrian_German "Austrian German"), which marked his speech throughout his life.[21][22][23] The family returned to Austria and settled in [Leonding](/wiki/Leonding "Leonding") on 9 May 1894,[24] and in June 1895, Alois retired to Hafeld, near [Lambach](/wiki/Lambach "Lambach"), where he farmed and kept bees. Hitler attended _[Volksschule](/wiki/Volksschule "Volksschule")_ (a state-funded primary school) in nearby [Fischlham](/wiki/Fischlham "Fischlham").[25][26]

The move to Hafeld coincided with the onset of intense father–son conflicts caused by Hitler's refusal to conform to the strict discipline of his school.[27] Alois tried to browbeat his son into obedience, while Adolf did his best to be the opposite of whatever his father wanted.[28] Alois would also beat his son, although his mother tried to protect him from regular beatings.[29]

Alois Hitler's farming efforts at Hafeld were unsuccessful, and in 1897, the family moved to Lambach. The eight-year-old Hitler took singing lessons, sang in the church choir, and even considered becoming a priest.[30] In 1898, the family returned permanently to Leonding. Hitler was deeply affected by the death of his younger brother Edmund in 1900 from [measles](/wiki/Measles "Measles"). Hitler transformed from a confident, outgoing, and conscientious student to a morose, detached boy who frequently clashed with his father and teachers.[31] [Paula Hitler](/wiki/Paula_Hitler "Paula Hitler") recalled that Adolf was a teenage bully who would often slap her.[29]

Alois had made a successful career in the customs bureau and wanted his son to follow in his footsteps.[32] Hitler later dramatised an episode from this period when his father took him to visit a customs office, depicting it as an event that gave rise to an unforgiving antagonism between father and son, who were both strong-willed.[33][34][35] Ignoring his son's desire to attend a classical high school and become an artist, Alois sent Hitler to the _[Realschule](/wiki/Realschule "Realschule")_ in Linz in September 1900.[d][36] Hitler rebelled against this decision, and in _Mein Kampf_ states that he intentionally performed poorly in school, hoping that once his father saw "what little progress I was making at the technical school he would let me devote myself to my dream".[37]

[](/wiki/File:Alois_Hitler_in_his_last_years_2.jpg)

Hitler's father, [Alois](/wiki/Alois_Hitler "Alois Hitler"), c. 1900

[](/wiki/File:Klara_Hitler.jpg)

Hitler's mother, [Klara](/wiki/Klara_Hitler "Klara Hitler"), 1870s

Like many Austrian Germans, Hitler began to develop [German nationalist](/wiki/German_nationalist "German nationalist") ideas from a young age.[38] He expressed loyalty only to Germany, despising the declining [Habsburg monarchy](/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy "Habsburg monarchy") and its rule over an ethnically diverse empire.[39][40] Hitler and his friends used the greeting "Heil", and sang the "[Deutschlandlied](/wiki/Deutschlandlied "Deutschlandlied")" instead of the [Austrian Imperial anthem](/wiki/Gott_erhalte_Franz_den_Kaiser#Austria-Hungary "Gott erhalte Franz den Kaiser").[41] After Alois's sudden death on 3 January 1903, Hitler's performance at school deteriorated, and his mother allowed him to leave.[42] He enrolled at the _Realschule_ in [Steyr](/wiki/Steyr "Steyr") in September 1904, where his behaviour and performance improved.[43] In 1905, after passing a repeat of the final exam, Hitler left the school without any ambitions for further education or clear plans for a career.[44]

### Early adulthood in Vienna and Munich

See also: [Paintings by Adolf Hitler](/wiki/Paintings_by_Adolf_Hitler "Paintings by Adolf Hitler")

[](/wiki/File:Hitler_house_in_Leonding.jpg)The house in [Leonding, Austria](/wiki/Leonding "Leonding"), where Hitler spent his early adolescence [](/wiki/File:Adolf_Hitler_Der_Alte_Hof.jpg)_The Alter Hof in Munich_ , a watercolour painting by Hitler in 1914

In 1907, Hitler left Linz to live and study fine art in [Vienna](/wiki/Vienna "Vienna"), financed by an orphan's benefits and support from his mother. He applied for admission to the [Academy of Fine Arts Vienna](/wiki/Academy_of_Fine_Arts_Vienna "Academy of Fine Arts Vienna") but was rejected twice.[45][46] The [director](/wiki/Rector_\(academia\) "Rector \(academia\)") suggested Hitler should apply to the School of Architecture, but he lacked the necessary academic credentials because he had not finished secondary school.[47]

On 21 December 1907, his mother died of breast cancer at the age of 47; Hitler was 18 at the time. In 1909, Hitler ran out of money and was forced to live a [bohemian](/wiki/Bohemianism "Bohemianism") life in homeless shelters and the [Meldemannstraße dormitory](/wiki/Meldemannstra%C3%9Fe_dormitory "Meldemannstraße dormitory").[48][49] He earned money as a casual labourer and by painting and selling [watercolours](/wiki/Watercolours "Watercolours") of Vienna's sights.[45] During his time in Vienna, he pursued a growing passion for architecture and music, attending ten performances of _[Lohengrin](/wiki/Lohengrin_\(opera\) "Lohengrin \(opera\)")_ , his favourite of [Richard Wagner](/wiki/Richard_Wagner "Richard Wagner")'s operas.[50]

In Vienna, Hitler was first exposed to racist rhetoric.[51] Populists such as mayor [Karl Lueger](/wiki/Karl_Lueger "Karl Lueger") exploited the city's prevalent [antisemitic](/wiki/Antisemitic "Antisemitic") sentiment, occasionally also espousing German nationalist notions for political benefit. German nationalism was especially widespread in the [Mariahilf](/wiki/Mariahilf "Mariahilf") district, where Hitler then lived.[52] [Georg Ritter von Schönerer](/wiki/Georg_Ritter_von_Sch%C3%B6nerer "Georg Ritter von Schönerer") became a major influence on Hitler,[53] and he developed an admiration for [Martin Luther](/wiki/Martin_Luther "Martin Luther").[54]

Hitler read newspapers and pamphlets that promoted prejudice and warned of an influx of Eastern European Jews,[55] as well as writings by figures such as [Houston Stewart Chamberlain](/wiki/Houston_Stewart_Chamberlain "Houston Stewart Chamberlain"), [Charles Darwin](/wiki/Charles_Darwin "Charles Darwin"), [Friedrich Nietzsche](/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche"), [Gustave Le Bon](/wiki/Gustave_Le_Bon "Gustave Le Bon"), and [Arthur Schopenhauer](/wiki/Arthur_Schopenhauer "Arthur Schopenhauer").[56] During his years in Vienna he also developed strong [anti-Slavic sentiments](/wiki/Anti-Slavic_sentiment "Anti-Slavic sentiment").[57][58]

The origin and development of Hitler's antisemitism remain a matter of debate.[59] His friend [August Kubizek](/wiki/August_Kubizek "August Kubizek") claimed that Hitler was a "confirmed antisemite" before he left Linz.[60] However, the historian [Brigitte Hamann](/wiki/Brigitte_Hamann "Brigitte Hamann") describes Kubizek's claim as "problematical".[61] While Hitler states in _Mein Kampf_ that he first became an antisemite in Vienna,[62] [Reinhold Hanisch](/wiki/Reinhold_Hanisch "Reinhold Hanisch"), who helped him to sell his paintings, disagrees. Hitler had dealings with Jews while living in Vienna.[63][64][65] The historian [Richard J. Evans](/wiki/Richard_J._Evans "Richard J. Evans") states that "historians now generally agree that his notorious, murderous antisemitism emerged well after Germany's defeat [in World War I], as a product of the paranoid ["stab-in-the-back" explanation](/wiki/Stab-in-the-back_myth "Stab-in-the-back myth") for the catastrophe".[66]

Hitler received the final part of his father's estate in May 1913 and moved to [Munich](/wiki/Munich "Munich").[67] When he was conscripted into the [Austro-Hungarian Army](/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Army "Austro-Hungarian Army"),[68] he journeyed to [Salzburg](/wiki/Salzburg "Salzburg") on 5 February 1914 for medical assessment. After he was deemed unfit for service, he returned to Munich.[69] Hitler later claimed that he did not wish to serve the [Habsburg Empire](/wiki/Austria-Hungary "Austria-Hungary") because of the mixture of races in its army and his belief that the collapse of Austria-Hungary was imminent.[70]

### World War I

Main article: [Military career of Adolf Hitler](/wiki/Military_career_of_Adolf_Hitler "Military career of Adolf Hitler")

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_146-1974-082-44,_Adolf_Hitler_im_Ersten_Weltkrieg_retouched.jpg)Hitler (far right, seated) with [Bavarian Army](/wiki/Bavarian_Army "Bavarian Army") comrades from the Bavarian Reserve Infantry Regiment 16 (c. 1914–18)

In August 1914, at the outbreak of [World War I](/wiki/World_War_I "World War I"), Hitler was living in Munich and voluntarily enlisted in the [Bavarian Army](/wiki/Bavarian_Army "Bavarian Army").[71] According to a 1924 report by the Bavarian authorities, allowing Hitler to serve was most likely an administrative error, because as an Austrian citizen, he should have been returned to Austria.[71] Posted to the [Bavarian Reserve Infantry Regiment 16](/wiki/6th_Bavarian_Reserve_Division "6th Bavarian Reserve Division") (1st Company of the List Regiment),[71][72] he served as a dispatch [runner](/wiki/Runner_\(soldier\) "Runner \(soldier\)") on the [Western Front](/wiki/Western_Front_\(World_War_I\) "Western Front \(World War I\)") in France and Belgium,[73] spending nearly half his time at the regimental headquarters in [Fournes-en-Weppes](/wiki/Fournes-en-Weppes "Fournes-en-Weppes"), well behind the front lines.[74][75] In 1914, he was present at the [First Battle of Ypres](/wiki/First_Battle_of_Ypres "First Battle of Ypres")[76] and in that year was decorated for bravery, receiving the [Iron Cross](/wiki/Iron_Cross "Iron Cross"), Second Class.[76] During the war, he was saved by his commanding officer, [Fritz Wiedemann](/wiki/Fritz_Wiedemann "Fritz Wiedemann"), who pulled Hitler out of the rubble of a collapsed building while under heavy fire.[77]

During his service at headquarters, Hitler pursued his artistic interests, drawing cartoons and providing instructions for an army newspaper. During the [Battle of the Somme](/wiki/Battle_of_the_Somme "Battle of the Somme") in October 1916, he was wounded in the left thigh when a shell exploded in the dispatch runners' dugout.[76][78] Hitler spent almost two months recovering in hospital at [Beelitz](/wiki/Beelitz "Beelitz"), returning to his regiment on 5 March 1917.[79] He was present at the [Battle of Arras](/wiki/Battle_of_Arras_\(1917\) "Battle of Arras \(1917\)") of 1917 and the [Battle of Passchendaele](/wiki/Battle_of_Passchendaele "Battle of Passchendaele").[76] He received the black [Wound Badge](/wiki/Wound_Badge "Wound Badge") on 18 May 1918.[80] Three months later, in August 1918, on a recommendation by Lieutenant [Hugo Gutmann](/wiki/Hugo_Gutmann "Hugo Gutmann"), his Jewish superior, Hitler received the Iron Cross, First Class, a decoration rarely awarded at Hitler's _[Gefreiter](/wiki/Gefreiter "Gefreiter")_ rank.[81][82] On 15 October 1918, he was temporarily blinded in a [mustard gas](/wiki/Mustard_gas "Mustard gas") attack and was hospitalised in [Pasewalk](/wiki/Pasewalk "Pasewalk").[83] While there, Hitler learned of Germany's defeat, and, by his own account, suffered a second bout of blindness after receiving this news.[84]

Hitler described his role in World War I as "the greatest of all experiences" and was praised by his commanding officers for his bravery.[85] His wartime experience reinforced his German patriotism, and he was shocked by Germany's capitulation in November 1918.[86] His displeasure with the collapse of the war effort began to shape his ideology.[87] Like other German nationalists, he believed the _Dolchstoßlegende_ ([stab-in-the-back myth](/wiki/Stab-in-the-back_myth "Stab-in-the-back myth")), which claimed that the German army, "undefeated in the field", had been "stabbed in the back" on the [home front](/wiki/Home_front "Home front") by civilian leaders, Jews, [Marxists](/wiki/Marxists "Marxists"), and those who signed the [armistice](/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918 "Armistice of 11 November 1918") that ended the fighting—later dubbed the "November criminals".[88]

The [Treaty of Versailles](/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles "Treaty of Versailles") stipulated that Germany had to relinquish several of its territories and [demilitarise](/wiki/Demilitarise "Demilitarise") the [Rhineland](/wiki/Rhineland "Rhineland"). The treaty imposed economic sanctions and levied heavy reparations on the country. Many Germans saw the treaty as an unjust humiliation. They especially objected to [Article 231](/wiki/Article_231 "Article 231"), which they interpreted as declaring Germany responsible for the war.[89] The Versailles Treaty and the economic, social, and political conditions in Germany after the war were later exploited by Hitler for political gain.[90]

## Entry into politics

Main article: [Political views of Adolf Hitler](/wiki/Political_views_of_Adolf_Hitler "Political views of Adolf Hitler")

[](/wiki/File:Hitler%27s_DAP_membership_card.png)Hitler's [German Workers' Party](/wiki/German_Workers%27_Party "German Workers' Party") (DAP) membership card

After the war, Hitler returned to Munich.[91] Without formal education or career prospects, he remained in the Army.[92] In July 1919, he was appointed _Verbindungsmann_ (intelligence agent) of an _Aufklärungskommando_ (reconnaissance unit) of the _[Reichswehr](/wiki/Reichswehr "Reichswehr")_ , assigned to influence other soldiers and to infiltrate the [German Workers' Party](/wiki/German_Workers%27_Party "German Workers' Party") (DAP). At a DAP meeting on 12 September 1919, Party chairman [Anton Drexler](/wiki/Anton_Drexler "Anton Drexler") was impressed by Hitler's oratorical skills. He gave him a copy of his pamphlet _My Political Awakening_ , which contained antisemitic, nationalist, [anti-capitalist](/wiki/Anti-capitalist "Anti-capitalist"), and anti-Marxist ideas.[93] On the orders of his army superiors, Hitler applied to join the party,[94] and within a week was accepted as party member 555 (the party began counting membership at 500 to give the impression they were a much larger party).[95][96]

Hitler made his earliest known written statement about the [Jewish question](/wiki/Jewish_question "Jewish question") in a 16 September 1919 letter to Adolf Gemlich (now known as the [Gemlich letter](/wiki/Gemlich_letter "Gemlich letter")). In the letter, Hitler argues that the aim of the government "must unshakably be the removal of the Jews altogether".[97] At the DAP, Hitler met [Dietrich Eckart](/wiki/Dietrich_Eckart "Dietrich Eckart"), one of the party's founders and a member of the occult [Thule Society](/wiki/Thule_Society "Thule Society").[98] Eckart became Hitler's mentor, exchanging ideas with him and introducing him to a wide range of Munich society.[99] To increase its appeal, the DAP changed its name to the _Nationalsozialistische Deutsche Arbeiterpartei_ (National Socialist German Workers' Party (NSDAP), now known as the "[Nazi Party](/wiki/Nazi_Party "Nazi Party")").[100] Hitler designed the party's banner of a [swastika](/wiki/Swastika "Swastika") in a white circle on a red background.[101]

Hitler was discharged from the Army on 31 March 1920 and began working full-time for the party.[102] The party headquarters was in Munich, a centre for anti-government German nationalists determined to eliminate Marxism and undermine the [Weimar Republic](/wiki/Weimar_Republic "Weimar Republic").[103]

[Friedrich Reck-Malleczewen](/wiki/Friedrich_Reck-Malleczewen "Friedrich Reck-Malleczewen") commented in his 1947 book _[Diary of a Man in Despair](/wiki/Diary_of_a_Man_in_Despair "Diary of a Man in Despair")_ : 

> He had come to a house where he had never been before, wearing gaiters, a floppy, wide-brimmed hat, and carrying a riding whip. ... Eventually, he managed to launch into a speech. He talked on and on, endlessly. He preached. He went on at us like a division chaplain in the Army. We did not in the least contradict him, or venture to differ in any way, but he began to bellow at us. The servants thought we were being attacked and rushed in to defend us. When he had gone, we sat silently, confused and not at all amused. There was a feeling of dismay, as when on a train you suddenly find you are sharing a compartment with a psychotic.[104]

In February 1921, already highly effective at [crowd manipulation](/wiki/Crowd_manipulation "Crowd manipulation"), Hitler spoke to a crowd of over 6,000.[105] To publicise the meeting, two truckloads of party supporters drove around Munich waving swastika flags and distributing leaflets. Hitler soon gained notoriety for his rowdy [polemic](/wiki/Polemic "Polemic") speeches against the Treaty of Versailles, rival politicians, and especially against Marxists and Jews.[106]

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_102-10460,_Adolf_Hitler,_Rednerposen.jpg)Hitler poses for the camera in September 1930.

In June 1921, while Hitler and Eckart were on a fundraising trip to Berlin, a mutiny broke out within the Nazi Party in Munich. Members of its executive committee wanted to merge with the Nuremberg-based [German Socialist Party](/wiki/German_Socialist_Party "German Socialist Party") (DSP).[107] Hitler returned to Munich on 11 July and angrily tendered his resignation. The committee members realised that the resignation of their leading public figure and speaker would mean the end of the party.[108] Hitler announced he would rejoin on the condition that he would replace Drexler as party chairman, and that the party headquarters would remain in Munich.[109] The committee agreed, and he rejoined the party on 26 July as member 3,680. Hitler continued to face some opposition within the Nazi Party. Opponents of Hitler in the leadership had [Hermann Esser](/wiki/Hermann_Esser "Hermann Esser") expelled from the party, and they printed 3,000 copies of a pamphlet attacking Hitler as a traitor to the party.[109][e] In the following days, Hitler spoke to several large audiences and defended himself and Esser, to thunderous applause. His strategy proved successful, and at a special party congress on 29 July, he was granted absolute power as party chairman, succeeding Drexler, by a vote of 533 to 1.[110]

Hitler's vitriolic beer hall speeches began attracting regular audiences. A [demagogue](/wiki/Demagogue "Demagogue"),[111] he became adept at using populist themes, including the use of [scapegoats](/wiki/Scapegoat "Scapegoat"), who were blamed for his listeners' economic hardships.[112][113][114] Hitler used personal magnetism and an understanding of [crowd psychology](/wiki/Crowd_psychology "Crowd psychology") to his advantage while engaged in public speaking.[115][116] Historians have noted the hypnotic effect of his rhetoric on large audiences, and of his eyes in small groups.[117] [Alfons Heck](/wiki/Alfons_Heck "Alfons Heck"), a former member of the [Hitler Youth](/wiki/Hitler_Youth "Hitler Youth"), recalled: 

> We erupted into a frenzy of nationalistic pride that bordered on hysteria. For minutes on end, we shouted at the top of our lungs, with tears streaming down our faces: _Sieg Heil, Sieg Heil, Sieg Heil!_ From that moment on, I belonged to Adolf Hitler body and soul.[118]

Early followers included [Rudolf Hess](/wiki/Rudolf_Hess "Rudolf Hess"), the former air force ace [Hermann Göring](/wiki/Hermann_G%C3%B6ring "Hermann Göring"), and the army captain [Ernst Röhm](/wiki/Ernst_R%C3%B6hm "Ernst Röhm"). Röhm became head of the Nazis' paramilitary organisation, the _[Sturmabteilung](/wiki/Sturmabteilung "Sturmabteilung")_ (SA, "Stormtroopers"), which protected meetings and attacked political opponents. A critical influence on Hitler's thinking during this period was the _[Aufbau Vereinigung](/wiki/Aufbau_Vereinigung "Aufbau Vereinigung")_ ,[119] a conspiratorial group of [White Russian](/wiki/White_%C3%A9migr%C3%A9 "White émigré") exiles and early Nazis. The group, financed with funds channelled from wealthy industrialists, introduced Hitler to the idea of a Jewish conspiracy, linking international finance with [Bolshevism](/wiki/Bolshevism "Bolshevism").[120]

The programme of the Nazi Party was laid out in their [25-point programme](/wiki/25-point_programme "25-point programme") on 24 February 1920. This did not represent a coherent ideology, but was a conglomeration of received ideas which had currency in the _[völkisch](/wiki/V%C3%B6lkisch "Völkisch")_ [pan-Germanic](/wiki/Pan-Germanic "Pan-Germanic") movement, such as [ultranationalism](/wiki/Ultranationalism "Ultranationalism"), opposition to the [Treaty of Versailles](/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles "Treaty of Versailles"), distrust of [capitalism](/wiki/Capitalism "Capitalism"), as well as some [socialist](/wiki/Socialist "Socialist") ideas. For Hitler, the most important aspect of it was its strong [antisemitic](/wiki/Antisemitic "Antisemitic") stance. He also perceived the programme as primarily a basis for propaganda and for attracting people to the party.[121]

### Beer Hall Putsch and Landsberg Prison

Main article: [Beer Hall Putsch](/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch "Beer Hall Putsch")

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_102-00344A,_M%C3%BCnchen,_nach_Hitler-Ludendorff_Prozess.jpg)Defendants in the [Beer Hall Putsch](/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch "Beer Hall Putsch") trial, 1 April 1924. From left to right: [Heinz Pernet](/wiki/Heinz_Pernet "Heinz Pernet"), [Friedrich Weber](/wiki/Friedrich_Weber_\(veterinarian\) "Friedrich Weber \(veterinarian\)"), [Wilhelm Frick](/wiki/Wilhelm_Frick "Wilhelm Frick"), [Hermann Kriebel](/wiki/Hermann_Kriebel "Hermann Kriebel"), [Erich Ludendorff](/wiki/Erich_Ludendorff "Erich Ludendorff"), Hitler, [Wilhelm Brückner](/wiki/Wilhelm_Br%C3%BCckner "Wilhelm Brückner"), [Ernst Röhm](/wiki/Ernst_R%C3%B6hm "Ernst Röhm"), and [Robert Wagner](/wiki/Robert_Heinrich_Wagner "Robert Heinrich Wagner"). [](/wiki/File:Mein_Kampf_dust_jacket.jpeg)The [dust jacket](/wiki/Dust_jacket "Dust jacket") of _[Mein Kampf](/wiki/Mein_Kampf "Mein Kampf")_ 's 1926–28 edition, which Hitler authored in 1925

In 1923, Hitler enlisted the help of World War I General [Erich Ludendorff](/wiki/Erich_Ludendorff "Erich Ludendorff") for an attempted coup known as the [Beer Hall Putsch](/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch "Beer Hall Putsch"). The Nazi Party used [Italian Fascism](/wiki/Italian_Fascism "Italian Fascism") as a model for their appearance and policies. Hitler wanted to emulate [Benito Mussolini](/wiki/Benito_Mussolini "Benito Mussolini")'s [March on Rome](/wiki/March_on_Rome "March on Rome") in 1922 by staging his own coup in Bavaria, to be followed by a challenge to the government in Berlin. Hitler and Ludendorff sought the support of _Staatskommissar_ (State Commissioner) [Gustav Ritter von Kahr](/wiki/Gustav_Ritter_von_Kahr "Gustav Ritter von Kahr"), Bavaria's _de facto_ ruler. However, Kahr, along with Police Chief [Hans Ritter von Seisser](/wiki/Hans_Ritter_von_Seisser "Hans Ritter von Seisser") and Reichswehr General [Otto von Lossow](/wiki/Otto_von_Lossow "Otto von Lossow"), wanted to install a nationalist dictatorship without Hitler.[122]

On 8 November 1923, Hitler and the SA stormed a public meeting of 3,000 people organised by Kahr in the [Bürgerbräukeller](/wiki/B%C3%BCrgerbr%C3%A4ukeller "Bürgerbräukeller"), a beer hall in Munich. Interrupting Kahr's speech, he announced that the national revolution had begun and declared the formation of a new government with Ludendorff.[123] Retiring to a back room, Hitler, with his pistol drawn, demanded and subsequently received the support of Kahr, Seisser, and Lossow.[123] Hitler's forces initially succeeded in occupying the local Reichswehr and police headquarters, but Kahr and his cohorts quickly withdrew their support. Neither the Army nor the state police joined forces with Hitler.[124] The next day, Hitler and his followers marched from the beer hall to the [Bavarian War Ministry](/wiki/Bavarian_War_Ministry "Bavarian War Ministry") to overthrow the Bavarian government, but police dispersed them.[125] In the failed coup, 16 Nazi Party members and four police officers were killed.[126]

Hitler fled to the home of [Ernst Hanfstaengl](/wiki/Ernst_Hanfstaengl "Ernst Hanfstaengl") and by some accounts contemplated suicide.[127] He was depressed but calm when arrested on 11 November 1923 for [high treason](/wiki/High_treason "High treason").[128] His trial before the special [People's Court](/wiki/People%27s_Court_\(Bavaria\) "People's Court \(Bavaria\)") in Munich began in February 1924,[129] and [Alfred Rosenberg](/wiki/Alfred_Rosenberg "Alfred Rosenberg") became the temporary leader of the Nazi Party. On 1 April, Hitler was sentenced to five years' _[Festungshaft](/wiki/Festungshaft "Festungshaft")_ ('fortress confinement') at [Landsberg Prison](/wiki/Landsberg_Prison "Landsberg Prison").[130] There, he received friendly treatment from the guards and was allowed mail from supporters and regular visits by party comrades. Pardoned by the Bavarian Supreme Court, he was released from jail on 20 December 1924, against the state prosecutor's objections.[131] Including time on remand, Hitler served just over one year in prison.[132]

While at Landsberg, Hitler dictated most of the first volume of _Mein Kampf_ (lit. 'My Struggle'; originally titled _Four and a Half Years of Struggle against Lies, Stupidity, and Cowardice_) at first to his chauffeur, [Emil Maurice](/wiki/Emil_Maurice "Emil Maurice"), and then to his deputy, [Rudolf Hess](/wiki/Rudolf_Hess "Rudolf Hess").[132][133] The book, dedicated to Thule Society member Dietrich Eckart, was an autobiography and exposition of his ideology. The book laid out Hitler's plans for territorial expansion as well as transforming German society into a dictatorship based on race. Throughout the book, Jews are equated with "germs" and presented as the "international poisoners" of society. According to Hitler's ideology, the only solution was their extermination. While Hitler did not describe exactly how this was to be accomplished, his "inherent genocidal thrust is undeniable", according to [Ian Kershaw](/wiki/Ian_Kershaw "Ian Kershaw").[134]

Published in two volumes in 1925 and 1926, _Mein Kampf_ sold 228,000 copies between 1925 and 1932. One million copies were sold in 1933, Hitler's first year in office.[135] Shortly before Hitler was eligible for parole, the Bavarian government attempted to have him deported to Austria.[136] The Austrian federal chancellor rejected the request on the specious grounds that his service in the German Army made his Austrian citizenship void.[137] In response, Hitler formally renounced his Austrian citizenship on 7 April 1925.[137]

### Rebuilding the Nazi Party

At the time of Hitler's release from prison, politics in Germany had become less combative, and the economy had improved, limiting Hitler's opportunities for political agitation. As a result of the failed Beer Hall Putsch, the Nazi Party and its affiliated organisations were banned in Bavaria. In a meeting with the Prime Minister of Bavaria, [Heinrich Held](/wiki/Heinrich_Held "Heinrich Held"), on 4 January 1925, Hitler agreed to respect the state's authority and promised that he would seek political power only through the democratic process. The meeting paved the way for the ban on the Nazi Party to be lifted on 16 February.[138]

However, after an inflammatory speech he gave on 27 February, Hitler was barred from public speaking by the Bavarian authorities, a ban that remained in place until 1927.[139][140] To advance his political ambitions despite the ban, Hitler appointed [Gregor Strasser](/wiki/Gregor_Strasser "Gregor Strasser"), [Otto Strasser](/wiki/Otto_Strasser "Otto Strasser"), and [Joseph Goebbels](/wiki/Joseph_Goebbels "Joseph Goebbels") to organise and enlarge the Nazi Party in northern Germany. Gregor Strasser steered a more independent political course, emphasising the socialist elements of the party's programme.[141]

The stock market in the United States [crashed on 24 October 1929](/wiki/Wall_Street_crash_of_1929 "Wall Street crash of 1929"). The impact in Germany was dire: millions became unemployed, and several major banks collapsed. Hitler and the Nazi Party prepared to take advantage of the emergency to gain support for their party. They promised to repudiate the Versailles Treaty, strengthen the economy, and provide jobs.[142]

Hitler discussed the name of the Nazi Party in October 1923: 

> "Why," I asked Hitler, "do you call yourself a National Socialist, since your party program is the very antithesis of that commonly accredited to socialism?" "Socialism," he retorted, putting down his cup of tea, pugnaciously, "is the science of dealing with the common weal. Communism is not Socialism. Marxism is not Socialism. The Marxians have stolen the term and confused its meaning. I shall take Socialism away from the Socialists. Socialism is an ancient Aryan, Germanic institution. ... We demand the fulfilment of the just claims of the productive classes by the state based on race solidarity. To us, state and race are one."[143]

## Rise to power

Main article: [Adolf Hitler's rise to power](/wiki/Adolf_Hitler%27s_rise_to_power "Adolf Hitler's rise to power")

Nazi Party election results[144] Election  | Total votes  | % votes  | Reichstag seats  | Notes   
---|---|---|---|---  
[May 1924](/wiki/May_1924_German_federal_election "May 1924 German federal election") | 1,918,300 | 6.5 | 32 | Hitler in prison   
[December 1924](/wiki/December_1924_German_federal_election "December 1924 German federal election") | 907,300 | 3.0 | 14 | Hitler released from prison   
[May 1928](/wiki/1928_German_federal_election "1928 German federal election") | 810,100 | 2.6 | 12 |   
[September 1930](/wiki/1930_German_federal_election "1930 German federal election") | 6,409,600 | 18.3 | 107 | After the financial crisis   
[July 1932](/wiki/July_1932_German_federal_election "July 1932 German federal election") | 13,745,000 | 37.3 | 230 | After Hitler was candidate for presidency   
[November 1932](/wiki/November_1932_German_federal_election "November 1932 German federal election") | 11,737,000 | 33.1 | 196 |   
[March 1933](/wiki/March_1933_German_federal_election "March 1933 German federal election") | 17,277,180 | 43.9 | 288 | Only partially free during Hitler's term as chancellor of Germany   
  
### Brüning administration

The Great Depression provided a political opportunity for Hitler. Germans were ambivalent about the [parliamentary republic](/wiki/Parliamentary_republic "Parliamentary republic"), which faced challenges from [right-](/wiki/Far-right_politics "Far-right politics") and [left-wing extremists](/wiki/Far-left_politics "Far-left politics"). The moderate political parties were increasingly unable to stem the tide of extremism, and the [German referendum of 1929](/wiki/1929_German_referendum "1929 German referendum") helped to elevate Nazi ideology.[145] The elections of September 1930 resulted in the break-up of a [grand coalition](/wiki/Grand_coalition "Grand coalition") and its replacement with a minority cabinet. Its leader, chancellor [Heinrich Brüning](/wiki/Heinrich_Br%C3%BCning "Heinrich Brüning") of the [Centre Party](/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Germany\) "Centre Party \(Germany\)"), governed through [emergency decrees](/wiki/Emergency_powers "Emergency powers") from President Paul von Hindenburg. Governance by decree became the new norm, paving the way for authoritarian forms of government.[146] The Nazi Party rose from obscurity to win 18.3 per cent of the vote and 107 parliamentary seats in the 1930 election, becoming the second-largest party in parliament.[147]

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_119-0289,_M%C3%BCnchen,_Hitler_bei_Einweihung_%22Braunes_Haus%22.jpg)Hitler and Nazi Party treasurer [Franz Xaver Schwarz](/wiki/Franz_Xaver_Schwarz "Franz Xaver Schwarz") at the dedication of the renovation of the Palais Barlow on [Brienner Straße](/wiki/Brienner_Stra%C3%9Fe_\(Munich\) "Brienner Straße \(Munich\)") in Munich into the [Brown House](/wiki/Brown_House,_Munich "Brown House, Munich") headquarters, December 1930

Hitler made a prominent appearance at the trial of two Reichswehr officers, Lieutenants Richard Scheringer and [Hanns Ludin](/wiki/Hanns_Ludin "Hanns Ludin"), in late 1930. Both were charged with membership in the Nazi Party, at that time illegal for Reichswehr personnel.[148] The prosecution argued that the Nazi Party was extremist, prompting defence lawyer Hans Frank to call on Hitler to testify.[149] On 25 September 1930, Hitler testified that his party would pursue political power solely through democratic elections,[150] which won him many supporters in the officer corps.[151]

Brüning's austerity measures brought little economic improvement and were extremely unpopular.[152] Hitler exploited this by targeting his political messages specifically at people who had been affected by the inflation of the 1920s and the Depression, such as farmers, war veterans, and the middle class.[153]

Although Hitler had terminated his Austrian citizenship in 1925, he did not acquire German citizenship for almost seven years. This meant that he was [stateless](/wiki/Statelessness "Statelessness"), legally unable to run for public office, and still faced the risk of deportation.[154] On 25 February 1932, the interior minister of [Brunswick](/wiki/Free_State_of_Brunswick "Free State of Brunswick"), [Dietrich Klagges](/wiki/Dietrich_Klagges "Dietrich Klagges"), who was a member of the Nazi Party, appointed Hitler as administrator for the state's delegation to the [Reichsrat](/wiki/Reichsrat_\(Germany\) "Reichsrat \(Germany\)") in Berlin, making Hitler a citizen of Brunswick,[155] and thus of Germany.[156]

Hitler ran against Hindenburg in the [1932 presidential election](/wiki/1932_German_presidential_election "1932 German presidential election"). A speech to the Industry Club in [Düsseldorf](/wiki/D%C3%BCsseldorf "Düsseldorf") on 27 January 1932 won him support from many of Germany's most powerful industrialists.[157] Hindenburg had support from various nationalist, monarchist, Catholic, and [republican](/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism") parties, and some [Social Democrats](/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Germany "Social Democratic Party of Germany"). Hitler used the campaign slogan "_Hitler über Deutschland_ " ("Hitler over Germany"), a reference to his political ambitions and his campaigning by aircraft.[158] He was one of the first politicians to use aircraft travel for campaigning and used it effectively.[159][160] Hitler came in second in both rounds of the election, garnering more than 35 per cent of the vote in the final election. Although he lost to Hindenburg, this election established Hitler as a strong force in German politics.[161]

### Appointment as chancellor

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_146-1972-026-11,_Macht%C3%BCbernahme_Hitlers.jpg)Hitler, at a window of the [Reich Chancellery](/wiki/Reich_Chancellery "Reich Chancellery"), receives an ovation on the evening of his inauguration as [chancellor](/wiki/Chancellor_of_Germany "Chancellor of Germany"), 30 January 1933

The absence of an effective government prompted two influential politicians, Franz von Papen and [Alfred Hugenberg](/wiki/Alfred_Hugenberg "Alfred Hugenberg"), along with several other industrialists and businessmen, to write a letter to Hindenburg. The signers urged Hindenburg to appoint Hitler as leader of a government "independent from parliamentary parties", which could turn into a movement that would "enrapture millions of people".[162][163]

Hindenburg reluctantly agreed to appoint Hitler as chancellor after two further parliamentary elections—in July and November 1932—had not resulted in the formation of a majority government. Hitler headed a short-lived coalition government formed by the Nazi Party (which had the most seats in the Reichstag) and Hugenberg's party, the [German National People's Party](/wiki/German_National_People%27s_Party "German National People's Party") (DNVP). On 30 January 1933, the new cabinet was sworn in during a brief ceremony in Hindenburg's office. The Nazi Party gained three posts: Hitler was named chancellor, [Wilhelm Frick](/wiki/Wilhelm_Frick "Wilhelm Frick") Minister of the Interior, and Hermann Göring Minister of the Interior for Prussia.[164] Hitler had insisted on the ministerial positions as a way to gain control over the police in much of Germany.[165]

### Reichstag fire and March elections

Main article: [Reichstag fire](/wiki/Reichstag_fire "Reichstag fire")

As chancellor, Hitler worked against attempts by the Nazi Party's opponents to build a majority government. Because of the political stalemate, he asked Hindenburg to again dissolve the Reichstag, and elections were scheduled for early March. On 27 February 1933, the [Reichstag building was set on fire](/wiki/Reichstag_fire "Reichstag fire"). Göring blamed a communist plot, as the Dutch communist [Marinus van der Lubbe](/wiki/Marinus_van_der_Lubbe "Marinus van der Lubbe") was found in incriminating circumstances inside the burning building.[166] Until the 1960s, some historians, including [William L. Shirer](/wiki/William_L._Shirer "William L. Shirer") and [Alan Bullock](/wiki/Alan_Bullock "Alan Bullock"), thought the Nazi Party was responsible;[167][168] now the view of most historians is van der Lubbe started the fire alone.[169]

At Hitler's urging, Hindenburg responded by signing the [Reichstag Fire Decree](/wiki/Reichstag_Fire_Decree "Reichstag Fire Decree") of 28 February, drafted by the Nazis, which suspended basic rights and allowed detention without trial. The decree was permitted under [Article 48](/wiki/Article_48_of_the_Weimar_Constitution "Article 48 of the Weimar Constitution") of the Weimar Constitution, which gave the president the power to take emergency measures to protect public safety and order.[170] Activities of the [German Communist Party](/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Germany "Communist Party of Germany") (KPD) were suppressed, and 4,000 KPD members were arrested.[171]

In addition to political campaigning, the Nazi Party engaged in paramilitary violence and the spread of anti-communist propaganda, in the days preceding [the election](/wiki/March_1933_German_federal_election "March 1933 German federal election"). On election day, 6 March 1933, the Nazi's share of the vote increased to 44%, and the party acquired the largest number of seats in parliament. Hitler's party failed to secure an absolute majority, necessitating another coalition with the DNVP.[172]

### Day of Potsdam and the Enabling Act

Main article: [Enabling Act of 1933](/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1933 "Enabling Act of 1933")

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-S38324,_Tag_von_Potsdam,_Adolf_Hitler,_Paul_v._Hindenburg.jpg)Hitler and [Paul von Hindenburg](/wiki/Paul_von_Hindenburg "Paul von Hindenburg") shaking hands on the Day of Potsdam, 21 March 1933

On 21 March 1933, the new _Reichstag_ was constituted with an opening ceremony at the [Garrison Church](/wiki/Garrison_Church,_Potsdam "Garrison Church, Potsdam") in [Potsdam](/wiki/Potsdam "Potsdam"). This "Day of Potsdam" was held to demonstrate unity between the Nazi movement and the old [Prussian](/wiki/Prussia "Prussia") elite and military. Hitler appeared in a [morning coat](/wiki/Morning_coat "Morning coat") and humbly greeted Hindenburg.[173][174]

To achieve full political control despite not having an absolute majority in parliament, Hitler's government brought the _Ermächtigungsgesetz_ (Enabling Act) to a vote in the newly elected _Reichstag_. The Act—officially titled the _Gesetz zur Behebung der Not von Volk und Reich_ ("Law to Remedy the Distress of People and Reich")—gave Hitler's cabinet the power to enact laws without the consent of the Reichstag for four years. These laws could (with certain exceptions) deviate from the constitution.[175]

Since it would affect the constitution, the Enabling Act required a two-thirds majority to pass. Leaving nothing to chance, the Nazis used the provisions of the Reichstag Fire Decree to arrest all 81 Communist deputies (in spite of their virulent campaign against the party, the Nazis had allowed the KPD to contest the election)[176] and prevent several Social Democrats from attending.[177]

On 23 March 1933, the _Reichstag_ assembled at the [Kroll Opera House](/wiki/Kroll_Opera_House "Kroll Opera House") under turbulent circumstances. Ranks of SA men served as guards inside the building, while large groups outside, opposing the proposed legislation, shouted slogans and threats towards the arriving members of parliament.[178] After Hitler verbally promised Centre party leader [Ludwig Kaas](/wiki/Ludwig_Kaas "Ludwig Kaas") that Hindenburg would retain his power of veto, Kaas announced the Centre Party would support the Enabling Act. The Act was passed by a vote of 444–94, with all parties except the Social Democrats voting in favour. The Enabling Act, along with the Reichstag Fire Decree, transformed Hitler's government into a _de facto_ legal dictatorship.[179]

### Dictatorship

> At the risk of appearing to talk nonsense, I tell you that the National Socialist movement will go on for 1,000 years! ... Don't forget how people laughed at me 15 years ago when I declared that one day I would govern Germany. They laugh now, just as foolishly, when I declare that I shall remain in power![180]

— Adolf Hitler to a British correspondent in Berlin, June 1934

Having achieved full control over the legislative and executive branches of government, Hitler and his allies began to suppress the remaining opposition. The Social Democratic Party was made illegal, and its assets were seized.[181] While many [trade union](/wiki/Trade_unions_in_Germany "Trade unions in Germany") delegates were in Berlin for May Day activities, SA stormtroopers occupied union offices around the country. On 2 May 1933, all trade unions were forced to dissolve, and their leaders were arrested. Some were sent to [concentration camps](/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps "Nazi concentration camps").[182] The [German Labour Front](/wiki/German_Labour_Front "German Labour Front") was formed as an umbrella organisation to represent all workers, administrators, and company owners, thus reflecting the concept of [Nazism](/wiki/Nazism "Nazism") in the spirit of Hitler's _[Volksgemeinschaft](/wiki/Volksgemeinschaft "Volksgemeinschaft")_ ("people's community").[183]

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_146-1990-048-29A,_Adolf_Hitler_retouched.jpg)In 1934, Hitler became Germany's head of state with the title of _[Führer und Reichskanzler](/wiki/F%C3%BChrer "Führer")_ (leader and chancellor of the Reich).

By the end of June, the other parties had been intimidated into disbanding. This included the Nazis' nominal coalition partner, the DNVP; with the SA's help, Hitler forced its leader, Hugenberg, to resign on 29 June. On 14 July 1933, the Nazi Party was declared the only legal political party in Germany.[183][181] The demands of the SA for more political and military power caused anxiety among military, industrial, and political leaders. In response, Hitler purged the entire SA leadership in the [Night of the Long Knives](/wiki/Night_of_the_Long_Knives "Night of the Long Knives"), which took place from 30 June to 2 July 1934.[184] Hitler targeted Ernst Röhm and other SA leaders who, along with several Hitler's political adversaries (such as Gregor Strasser and former chancellor [Kurt von Schleicher](/wiki/Kurt_von_Schleicher "Kurt von Schleicher")), were rounded up, arrested, and shot.[185] While the international community and some Germans were shocked by the killings, many in Germany believed Hitler was restoring order.[186]

Hindenburg died on 2 August 1934. On the previous day, the cabinet had enacted the [Law Concerning the Head of State of the German Reich](/wiki/Law_Concerning_the_Head_of_State_of_the_German_Reich "Law Concerning the Head of State of the German Reich").[2] This law stated that upon Hindenburg's death, the office of president would be abolished, and its powers merged with those of the chancellor. Hitler thus became head of state as well as head of government and was formally named as _Führer und Reichskanzler_ (Leader and Chancellor of the Reich),[1] although _Reichskanzler_ was eventually dropped.[187] With this action, Hitler eliminated the last legal remedy by which he could be removed from office.[188]

As head of state, Hitler became commander-in-chief of the armed forces. Immediately after Hindenburg's death, at the instigation of the leadership of the _Reichswehr_ , the traditional loyalty oath of soldiers was altered to [affirm loyalty to Hitler personally, by name](/wiki/Hitler_oath "Hitler oath"), rather than to the office of commander-in-chief (which was later renamed to supreme commander) or to Germany.[189] On 19 August, the merger of the presidency with the chancellorship was approved by 88 per cent of the electorate voting in a [plebiscite](/wiki/1934_German_referendum "1934 German referendum").[190]

[](/wiki/File:Standarte_Adolf_Hitlers.svg)[Hitler's personal standard](/wiki/Hitler%27s_personal_standard "Hitler's personal standard")

In early 1938, Hitler used blackmail to consolidate his hold over the military by instigating the [Blomberg–Fritsch affair](/wiki/Blomberg%E2%80%93Fritsch_affair "Blomberg–Fritsch affair"). Hitler forced his War Minister, Field Marshal [Werner von Blomberg](/wiki/Werner_von_Blomberg "Werner von Blomberg"), to resign by using a police dossier that showed that Blomberg's new wife had a record for prostitution.[191][192] Army commander Colonel-General [Werner von Fritsch](/wiki/Werner_von_Fritsch "Werner von Fritsch") was removed after the _[Schutzstaffel](/wiki/Schutzstaffel "Schutzstaffel")_ (SS) produced allegations that he had engaged in a homosexual relationship.[193] Both men had fallen into disfavour because they objected to Hitler's demand to make the _[Wehrmacht](/wiki/Wehrmacht "Wehrmacht")_ ready for war as early as 1938.[194] Hitler assumed Blomberg's title of Commander-in-Chief, thus taking personal command of the armed forces.[195] He replaced the Ministry of War with the _[Oberkommando der Wehrmacht](/wiki/Oberkommando_der_Wehrmacht "Oberkommando der Wehrmacht")_ (OKW), headed by General [Wilhelm Keitel](/wiki/Wilhelm_Keitel "Wilhelm Keitel"). On the same day, 16 generals were stripped of their commands, and 44 more were transferred; all were suspected of not being sufficiently pro-Nazi.[196] By early February 1938, 12 more generals had been removed.[197]

Hitler took care to give his dictatorship the appearance of legality. Many of his decrees were explicitly based on the Reichstag Fire Decree and hence on Article 48 of the Weimar Constitution. The _Reichstag_ renewed the Enabling Act twice, each time for four years.[198] While elections to the _Reichstag_ were still held (in 1933, 1936, and 1938), voters were presented with a single list of Nazis and pro-Nazi "guests" which received well over 90 per cent of the vote.[199] These sham elections were held in far-from-secret conditions; the Nazis threatened severe reprisals against anyone who did not vote or who voted against.[200]

## Nazi Germany

Main article: [Nazi Germany](/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany")

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_102-04062A,_N%C3%BCrnberg,_Reichsparteitag,_SA-_und_SS-Appell.jpg)Hitler, [Heinrich Himmler](/wiki/Heinrich_Himmler "Heinrich Himmler"), and [SA](/wiki/Sturmabteilung "Sturmabteilung") chief [Viktor Lutze](/wiki/Viktor_Lutze "Viktor Lutze") render the [Nazi salute](/wiki/Nazi_salute "Nazi salute") at the 1934 [Nuremberg Rally](/wiki/Nuremberg_rallies "Nuremberg rallies").

### Economy and culture

Main article: [Economy of Nazi Germany](/wiki/Economy_of_Nazi_Germany "Economy of Nazi Germany")

In August 1934, Hitler appointed _Reichsbank_ President [Hjalmar Schacht](/wiki/Hjalmar_Schacht "Hjalmar Schacht") as Minister of Economics, and in the following year, as Plenipotentiary for War Economy in charge of preparing the economy for war.[201] Reconstruction and rearmament were financed through [Mefo bills](/wiki/Mefo_bills "Mefo bills"), printing money, and seizing the assets of people arrested as [enemies of the state](/wiki/Enemies_of_the_state "Enemies of the state"), including Jews.[202] The number of unemployed fell from six million in 1932 to fewer than one million in 1936.[203] Hitler oversaw one of the largest infrastructure improvement campaigns in German history, leading to the construction of dams, [_autobahns_](/wiki/Autobahn "Autobahn"), railroads, and other civil works. Wages were slightly lower in the mid- to late 1930s compared with wages during the Weimar Republic, while the cost of living increased by 25 per cent.[204] The average workweek increased during the shift to a war economy; by 1939, the average German was working between 47 and 50 hours a week.[205]

Hitler's government sponsored [architecture](/wiki/Nazi_architecture "Nazi architecture") on an immense scale. [Albert Speer](/wiki/Albert_Speer "Albert Speer"), instrumental in implementing Hitler's classicist reinterpretation of German culture, was placed in charge of the [proposed architectural renovations of Berlin](/wiki/Welthauptstadt_Germania "Welthauptstadt Germania").[206] Despite a threatened [multi-nation boycott](/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics#Boycott_debate "1936 Summer Olympics"), Germany hosted the 1936 Olympic Games. Hitler [officiated](/wiki/List_of_people_who_have_opened_the_Olympic_Games "List of people who have opened the Olympic Games") at the opening ceremonies and attended events at both the [Winter Games](/wiki/1936_Winter_Olympics "1936 Winter Olympics") in [Garmisch-Partenkirchen](/wiki/Garmisch-Partenkirchen "Garmisch-Partenkirchen") and the [Summer Games](/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics "1936 Summer Olympics") in Berlin.[207]

### Rearmament and new alliances

Main articles: [Axis powers](/wiki/Axis_powers "Axis powers"), [Tripartite Pact](/wiki/Tripartite_Pact "Tripartite Pact"), and [German re-armament](/wiki/German_re-armament "German re-armament")

Hitler giving a speech at the [Krupp Factory](/wiki/Krupp_Factory "Krupp Factory") in [Essen, Germany](/wiki/Essen,_Germany "Essen, Germany"), 1935

In a meeting with German military leaders on 3 February 1933, Hitler spoke of "conquest for _Lebensraum_ in the East and its ruthless Germanisation" as his ultimate foreign policy objectives.[208] In March, Prince Bernhard Wilhelm von Bülow, secretary at the [Foreign Office](/wiki/Federal_Foreign_Office "Federal Foreign Office") (_Auswärtiges Amt_), issued a statement of major foreign policy aims: _[Anschluss](/wiki/Anschluss "Anschluss")_ with Austria, the restoration of Germany's national borders of 1914, rejection of military restrictions under the Treaty of Versailles, the return of the former German colonies in Africa, and a German zone of influence in Eastern Europe. Hitler found Bülow's goals to be too modest.[209] In speeches during this period, he stressed what he termed the peaceful goals of his policies and a willingness to work within international agreements.[210] At the first meeting of his cabinet in 1933, Hitler prioritised military spending over unemployment relief.[211]

Germany withdrew from the [League of Nations](/wiki/League_of_Nations "League of Nations") and the [World Disarmament Conference](/wiki/World_Disarmament_Conference "World Disarmament Conference") in October 1933.[212] In January 1935, over 90 per cent of the people of the [Saarland](/wiki/Saarland "Saarland"), then under League of Nations administration, [voted to unite with Germany](/wiki/1935_Saar_status_referendum "1935 Saar status referendum").[213] That March, Hitler announced an expansion of the Wehrmacht to 600,000 members—six times the number permitted by the Versailles Treaty—including development of an air force (_[Luftwaffe](/wiki/Luftwaffe "Luftwaffe")_) and an increase in the size of the navy (_[Kriegsmarine](/wiki/Kriegsmarine "Kriegsmarine")_). Britain, France, Italy, and the League of Nations condemned these violations of the Treaty but did nothing to stop them.[214][215] The [Anglo-German Naval Agreement](/wiki/Anglo-German_Naval_Agreement "Anglo-German Naval Agreement") (AGNA) of 18 June allowed German tonnage to increase to 35 per cent of that of the [Royal Navy](/wiki/Royal_Navy "Royal Navy"). Hitler called the signing of the AGNA "the happiest day of his life", believing that the agreement marked the beginning of the Anglo-German alliance he had predicted in _Mein Kampf_.[216] France and Italy were not consulted before the signing, directly undermining the League of Nations and setting the Treaty of Versailles on the path towards irrelevance.[217]

Germany [reoccupied](/wiki/Remilitarization_of_the_Rhineland "Remilitarization of the Rhineland") the demilitarised zone in the Rhineland in March 1936, in violation of the Versailles Treaty. Hitler also sent troops to Spain to support [Francisco Franco](/wiki/Francisco_Franco "Francisco Franco") and his [Nationalist faction](/wiki/Nationalist_faction_\(Spanish_Civil_War\) "Nationalist faction \(Spanish Civil War\)") during the [Spanish Civil War](/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War "Spanish Civil War") after receiving an appeal for help in July 1936. At the same time, Hitler continued his efforts to create an Anglo-German alliance.[218] In August 1936, in response to a growing economic crisis caused by his rearmament efforts, Hitler ordered Göring to implement a [Four Year Plan](/wiki/Four_Year_Plan "Four Year Plan") to prepare Germany for war within the next four years.[219] The plan envisaged an all-out struggle between "[Judaeo-Bolshevism](/wiki/Judeo-Bolshevism "Judeo-Bolshevism")" and German Nazism, which in Hitler's view required a committed effort of rearmament regardless of the economic costs.[220]

In October 1936, Count [Galeazzo Ciano](/wiki/Galeazzo_Ciano "Galeazzo Ciano"), foreign minister of Mussolini's government, visited Germany, where he signed a [Nine-Point Protocol](/wiki/Italo-German_protocol_of_23_October_1936 "Italo-German protocol of 23 October 1936") as an expression of _rapprochement_ and had a personal meeting with Hitler. On 1 November, Mussolini declared an "axis" between Germany and Italy.[221] On 25 November, Germany signed the [Anti-Comintern Pact](/wiki/Anti-Comintern_Pact "Anti-Comintern Pact") with [Japan](/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan"). Britain, China, Italy, and Poland were also invited to join the Anti-Comintern Pact, but only Italy signed in 1937. Hitler abandoned his plan of an Anglo-German alliance, blaming "inadequate" British leadership.[222] At a meeting in the [Reich Chancellery](/wiki/Reich_Chancellery "Reich Chancellery") with his foreign ministers and military chiefs that November, Hitler restated his intention of acquiring _Lebensraum_ for the German people. He ordered preparations for war in the East to begin as early as 1938 and no later than 1943. In the event of his death, the conference minutes, recorded as the [Hossbach Memorandum](/wiki/Hossbach_Memorandum "Hossbach Memorandum"), were to be regarded as his "political testament".[223] He felt that a severe decline in living standards in Germany as a result of the economic crisis could only be stopped by military aggression aimed at seizing Austria and [Czechoslovakia](/wiki/Czechoslovakia "Czechoslovakia").[224][225] Hitler urged quick action before Britain and France gained a permanent lead in the [arms race](/wiki/Arms_race "Arms race").[224] In early 1938, in the wake of the [Blomberg–Fritsch affair](/wiki/Blomberg%E2%80%93Fritsch_affair "Blomberg–Fritsch affair"), Hitler asserted control of the military-foreign policy apparatus, dismissing Neurath as foreign minister and appointing himself as War Minister.[219] From early 1938 onwards, Hitler was carrying out a foreign policy ultimately aimed at war.[226]

## World War II

### Early diplomatic successes

[](/wiki/File:Matsuoka_bes%C3%B8ger_Hitler.jpg)Hitler and the Japanese foreign minister, [Yōsuke Matsuoka](/wiki/Y%C5%8Dsuke_Matsuoka "Yōsuke Matsuoka"), at a meeting in Berlin in March 1941. In the background is [Joachim von Ribbentrop](/wiki/Joachim_von_Ribbentrop "Joachim von Ribbentrop").

#### Alliance with Japan

See also: [Germany–Japan relations](/wiki/Germany%E2%80%93Japan_relations "Germany–Japan relations")

In February 1938, on the advice of his newly appointed foreign minister, the strongly pro-Japanese [Joachim von Ribbentrop](/wiki/Joachim_von_Ribbentrop "Joachim von Ribbentrop"), Hitler ended the [Sino-German alliance](/wiki/Sino-German_cooperation_until_1941 "Sino-German cooperation until 1941") with the [Republic of China](/wiki/Republic_of_China_\(1912%E2%80%9349\) "Republic of China \(1912–49\)") to instead enter into an alliance with the more modern and powerful [Empire of Japan](/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan"). Hitler announced German recognition of [Manchukuo](/wiki/Manchukuo "Manchukuo"), the Japanese puppet state in [Manchuria](/wiki/Manchuria "Manchuria"), and renounced German claims to their former colonies in the Pacific held by Japan.[227] Hitler ordered an end to arms shipments to China and recalled all German officers working with the Chinese Army.[227] In retaliation, Chinese General [Chiang Kai-shek](/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek "Chiang Kai-shek") cancelled all Sino-German economic agreements, depriving the Germans of many Chinese raw materials.[228]

#### Austria and Czechoslovakia

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_137-004055,_Eger,_Besuch_Adolf_Hitlers.jpg)Hitler driven through a crowd in [Cheb](/wiki/Cheb "Cheb") (German: _Eger_), in the [Sudetenland](/wiki/Sudetenland "Sudetenland"), October 1938

On 12 March 1938, Hitler announced the unification of Austria with Germany in the _[Anschluss](/wiki/Anschluss "Anschluss")_.[229][230] Hitler then turned his attention to the [ethnic German](/wiki/Ethnic_German "Ethnic German") population of the [Sudetenland](/wiki/Sudetenland "Sudetenland") region of Czechoslovakia.[231] On 28–29 March 1938, Hitler held a series of secret meetings in Berlin with [Konrad Henlein](/wiki/Konrad_Henlein "Konrad Henlein") of the [Sudeten German Party](/wiki/Sudeten_German_Party "Sudeten German Party"), the largest of the ethnic German parties of the Sudetenland. The men agreed that Henlein would demand increased autonomy for [Sudeten Germans](/wiki/Sudeten_Germans "Sudeten Germans") from the Czechoslovak government, thus providing a pretext for German military action against Czechoslovakia. In April 1938, Henlein told the [foreign minister](/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Hungary\) "Minister of Foreign Affairs \(Hungary\)") of [Hungary](/wiki/Hungary "Hungary") that "whatever the Czech government might offer, he would always raise still higher demands ... he wanted to sabotage an understanding by any means because this was the only method to blow up Czechoslovakia quickly".[232] In private, Hitler considered the Sudeten issue unimportant; his real intention was a war of conquest against Czechoslovakia.[233]

In April, Hitler ordered the OKW to prepare for _[Fall Grün](/wiki/Fall_Gr%C3%BCn_\(Czechoslovakia\) "Fall Grün \(Czechoslovakia\)")_ (Case Green), the code name for an invasion of Czechoslovakia.[234] As a result of intense French and British diplomatic pressure, on 5 September, Czechoslovak President [Edvard Beneš](/wiki/Edvard_Bene%C5%A1 "Edvard Beneš") unveiled the "Fourth Plan" for constitutional reorganisation of his country, which agreed to most of Henlein's demands for Sudeten autonomy.[235] Henlein's party responded to Beneš's offer by instigating a series of violent clashes with the Czechoslovak police that led to the declaration of martial law in certain Sudeten districts.[236][237]

Germany was dependent on imported oil; a confrontation with Britain over the Czechoslovak dispute could curtail Germany's oil supplies. This forced Hitler to call off _Fall Grün_ , originally planned for 1 October 1938.[238] On 29 September, Hitler, [Neville Chamberlain](/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain "Neville Chamberlain"), [Édouard Daladier](/wiki/%C3%89douard_Daladier "Édouard Daladier"), and Mussolini attended a one-day conference in Munich that led to the [Munich Agreement](/wiki/Munich_Agreement "Munich Agreement"), which handed over the Sudetenland districts to Germany.[239][240]

Chamberlain was satisfied with the Munich conference, calling the outcome "[peace for our time](/wiki/Peace_for_our_time "Peace for our time")", while Hitler was angered about the missed opportunity for war in 1938;[241][242] he expressed his disappointment in a speech on 9 October in [Saarbrücken](/wiki/Saarbr%C3%BCcken "Saarbrücken").[243] In Hitler's view, the British-brokered peace, although favourable to the ostensible German demands, was a diplomatic defeat which spurred his intent of limiting British power to pave the way for the eastern expansion of Germany.[244][245] As a result of the summit, Hitler was selected _[Time](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ magazine's [Man of the Year](/wiki/Time_Person_of_the_Year "Time Person of the Year") for 1938.[246] In late 1938 and early 1939, the continuing economic crisis caused by rearmament forced Hitler to make major defence cuts.[247] In his "Export or die" [speech of 30 January 1939](/wiki/30_January_1939_Reichstag_speech "30 January 1939 Reichstag speech"), he called for an economic offensive to increase German foreign exchange holdings to pay for raw materials such as high-grade iron needed for military weapons.[247]

On 14 March 1939, under threat from Hungary, [Slovakia declared independence](/wiki/Slovak_Republic_\(1939%E2%80%931945\) "Slovak Republic \(1939–1945\)") and received protection from Germany.[248] The next day, in violation of the Munich Agreement and possibly as a result of the deepening economic crisis requiring additional assets,[249] Hitler ordered the Wehrmacht to [invade the Czech rump state](/wiki/Occupation_of_Czechoslovakia_\(1938%E2%80%931945\) "Occupation of Czechoslovakia \(1938–1945\)"), and from [Prague Castle](/wiki/Prague_Castle "Prague Castle") he proclaimed the territory a [German protectorate](/wiki/Protectorate_of_Bohemia_and_Moravia "Protectorate of Bohemia and Moravia").[250]

### Start of World War II

See also: [Causes of World War II](/wiki/Causes_of_World_War_II "Causes of World War II")

[](/wiki/File:Greater_Germanic_Reich.png)Boundaries of the Nazi-planned [Greater Germanic Reich](/wiki/Greater_Germanic_Reich "Greater Germanic Reich") [](/wiki/File:Benito_Mussolini_and_Adolf_Hitler.jpg)Hitler and Benito Mussolini stand together on a reviewing stand during Mussolini's official visit in Munich, 1937

In private discussions in 1939, Hitler declared Britain the main enemy to be defeated and that Poland's obliteration was a necessary prelude for that goal.[251] The eastern flank would be secured, and land would be added to Germany's _Lebensraum_.[252] Offended by the British "guarantee" on 31 March 1939 of Polish independence, he said, "I shall brew them a devil's drink".[253] In a speech in [Wilhelmshaven](/wiki/Wilhelmshaven "Wilhelmshaven") for the launch of the battleship [_Tirpitz_](/wiki/German_battleship_Tirpitz "German battleship Tirpitz") on 1 April, he threatened to denounce the [Anglo-German Naval Agreement](/wiki/Anglo-German_Naval_Agreement "Anglo-German Naval Agreement") if the British continued to guarantee Polish independence, which he perceived as an "encirclement" policy.[253] Poland was to either become a German [satellite state](/wiki/Satellite_state "Satellite state"), or it would be neutralised to secure the Reich's eastern flank and prevent a possible British blockade.[254]

Hitler initially favoured the idea of a satellite state, but upon its rejection by the Polish government, he decided to invade and made this the main foreign policy goal of 1939.[255] On 3 April, Hitler ordered the military to prepare for _[Fall Weiss](/wiki/Fall_Weiss_\(1939\) "Fall Weiss \(1939\)")_ ("Case White"), the plan for invading Poland on 25 August.[255] In a Reichstag speech on 28 April, he renounced both the Anglo-German Naval Agreement and the [German–Polish Non-Aggression Pact](/wiki/German%E2%80%93Polish_Non-Aggression_Pact "German–Polish Non-Aggression Pact").[256] Historians such as [William Carr](/wiki/William_Carr_\(historian\) "William Carr \(historian\)"), [Gerhard Weinberg](/wiki/Gerhard_Weinberg "Gerhard Weinberg"), and [Ian Kershaw](/wiki/Ian_Kershaw "Ian Kershaw") have argued that one reason for Hitler's rush to war was his fear of an early death. He had repeatedly claimed that he must lead Germany into war before he got too old, as his successors might lack his strength of will.[257][258][259] Hitler was concerned that a military attack against Poland could result in a premature war with Britain.[254][260] Hitler's foreign minister and former Ambassador to London, Joachim von Ribbentrop, assured him that neither Britain nor France would honour its commitments to Poland.[261][262] Accordingly, on 22 August 1939, Hitler ordered a military mobilisation against Poland.[263]

This plan required tacit Soviet support,[264] and the [non-aggression pact](/wiki/Non-aggression_pact "Non-aggression pact") (the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact "Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact")) between Germany and the [Soviet Union](/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"), led by [Joseph Stalin](/wiki/Joseph_Stalin "Joseph Stalin"), included a secret agreement to partition Poland between the two countries.[265] Contrary to Ribbentrop's prediction that Britain would sever Anglo-Polish ties, Britain and Poland signed the Anglo-Polish alliance on 25 August 1939. This, along with news from Italy that Mussolini would not honour the [Pact of Steel](/wiki/Pact_of_Steel "Pact of Steel"), prompted Hitler to postpone the attack on Poland from 25 August to 1 September.[266] Hitler unsuccessfully tried to manoeuvre the British into neutrality by offering them a non-aggression guarantee on 25 August; he then instructed Ribbentrop to present a last-minute peace plan with an impossibly short time limit in an effort to blame the imminent war on British and Polish inaction.[267][268]

On 1 September 1939, Germany [invaded western Poland](/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland "Invasion of Poland") under the pretext of having been denied claims to the [Free City of Danzig](/wiki/Free_City_of_Danzig "Free City of Danzig") and the right to extraterritorial roads across the [Polish Corridor](/wiki/Polish_Corridor "Polish Corridor"), which Germany had ceded under the Versailles Treaty.[269] In response, [Britain and France declared war](/wiki/British_and_French_declaration_of_war_on_Germany "British and French declaration of war on Germany") on Germany on 3 September, surprising Hitler and prompting him to angrily ask Ribbentrop, "Now what?"[270] Britain and France did not act on their declarations immediately, and on 17 September, Soviet forces invaded eastern Poland.[271]

[](/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-S55480,_Polen,_Parade_vor_Adolf_Hitler.jpg)Hitler reviews troops on the march during the [campaign against Poland](/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland "Invasion of Poland") (September 1939).

The fall of Poland was followed by what contemporary journalists dubbed the "[Phoney War](/wiki/Phoney_War "Phoney War")" or _Sitzkrieg_ ("sitting war"). Hitler instructed the two newly appointed [Gauleiters](/wiki/Gauleiter "Gauleiter") of north-western Poland, [Albert Forster](/wiki/Albert_Forster "Albert Forster") of [Reichsgau Danzig-West Prussia](/wiki/Reichsgau_Danzig-West_Prussia "Reichsgau Danzig-West Prussia") and [Arthur Greiser](/wiki/Arthur_Greiser "Arthur Greiser") of [Reichsgau Wartheland](/wiki/Reichsgau_Wartheland "Reichsgau Wartheland"), to [Germanise](/wiki/Germanise "Germanise") their areas, with "no questions asked" about how this was accomplished.[272] In Forster's area, ethnic Poles merely had to sign forms stating that they had German blood.[273] In contrast, Greiser agreed with Himmler and carried out an [ethnic cleansing](/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing "Ethnic cleansing") campaign towards Poles. Greiser soon complained that Forster was allowing thousands of Poles to be accepted as "racial" Germans and thus endangered German "racial purity".[272] Hitler refrained from getting involved. This inaction has been cited as an example of the theory of "working towards the Führer", in which Hitler issued vague instructions and expected his subordinates to develop policies independently.[272][274]

Another dispute pitched one side represented by [Heinrich Himmler](/wiki/Heinrich_Himmler "Heinrich Himmler") and Greiser, who championed ethnic cleansing in Poland, against another represented by Göring and Hans Frank ([governor-general](/wiki/General_Government "General Government") of occupied Poland), who called for turning Poland into the "granary" of the Reich. On 12 February 1940, the dispute was initially settled in favour of the Göring–Frank view, which ended the economically disruptive mass expulsions.
